But oftentimes when you start to get lots of different use states,

It can become difficult to keep track of and manage, especially because one use state may kind of depend a little bit on another one, like they may be slightly linked to each other. For example, the loading state for a fetch request is somewhat linked to the error state because you can't be both loading and error at the same time. So you kind of need to be able to transfer between those states. And also sometimes when you have your state, you have like distinct things you can do. In our counterexample, we have two things that our state can do. We can decrement our state, so decrease it by one.

Or we can increment it by increasing it by one. Those are the only two actions our state can perform. We can't do anything else other than that. So often when you have distinct actions you can perform on your state, it's best to use the use reducer hook because it's set up in a way where you can call specific actions on your state and it does all the transformation of the data for you. So with that exclamation out of the way, let me show you exactly how to use the use reducer hook. So we can come in here, we can say use reducer, and use reducer takes

So in our case, our default value is going to be initial count for our default state. Now this second initial count property you may think works exactly the same as use state, but it's slightly different in that use reducer actually accepts three parameters. So when you pass just two parameters, you have your reducer function and you have your initial state. This initial state cannot take a function at all. It can only take a value.

All I'm doing with this child component is passing these props down. This idea of passing props to a component, which are only then passed to another component, is something called prop drilling.

The whole idea behind that term means that you're just passing props down to a descendant that is a child of another component that doesn't even use those props at all. Generally, when you're doing this, this is considered a bad practice. It's fine to do this for like one, maybe two props every once in a while in your application, but when you start to have something like a dark mode or a toggle theme, which are going to be used everywhere in your application, it makes more sense to move to context, which allows you to pass down props to these grandchild components or any component that's nested inside of another component.

Without having to pass it through every single child in this prop drilling method. So let's take a look at how we can actually create a context because it's rather simple overall. All we need to do is up here inside of React, we can import this create context method right here. And then we just need to create a context outside of our function components. So it's just outside of any of your components. And we're going to call this our theme context. And we're going to set that equal to create context. And in here is where you pass it whatever default value you want to use.

In our case, we're just not going to pass anything along at all as our default value. Also, it's important when you define a consent context to use a capital letter at the front here, just because you're also going to use this as a component inside your React application. We can just slap down this one single line and we have access to all of that dark mode related functionality without having to pass it down through all of the different props. Now, this is obviously incredibly powerful, but there are some caveats you should keep in mind. And you shouldn't use this for all of your state. Obviously, if you need to pass down some props to a child and you don't need to pass them any further, just use props. That's what they're there for. But if you have some type of state that is going to be either global to your entire application, such as this theme context provider.

Or it's going to be global to a certain subset of your application. For example, if you have a section in your application that's dealing with like comments for a post, maybe you want to have that entire section wrapped inside of a context for dealing with your comments because it's fairly complex state and you have lots of different components and they all need access to that information. So you would wrap that one section of your application inside of a context that is providing that data for just dealing with comments.

This is a super common use case for any time that you have lots of data that you need to share with lots of different components that are all going to be various levels of nested inside of that one parent component. Honestly, the use context hook and just the whole idea of context inside of React is one of my favorite things that they added because again, it makes dealing with complex state that needs to go to lots of different components much easier, and it just makes sharing state so much easier. This hook combined with the use reducer hook.

means that for the most part, I don't think that Redux is needed anymore for most modern applications. Because Redux, the main things that it solved is it gave you reducer-like capabilities for handling your state, and it made it really easy to share state between all of your different components. But with use context and just context in general and the use reducer hook, it pretty much solves all those use cases.
